storage.\n *\n * <p>Rule 4 (identifiers and tokens): the token is 32 bytes from {@link SecureRandom} -- generated\n * here, never derived from the account, the time, or anything a caller supplies, and never accepted\n * as a caller-chosen value (no session fixation). Only {@code SHA-256(token)} is stored, so a\n * reader of the database cannot replay a session, and lookups are by that digest.\n *\n * <p>Rule 3: {@link #validate} is the only way to turn a token into an account id, and it checks\n * revocation, idle expiry and absolute expiry against this table -- server-side state. Anything it\n * cannot positively confirm returns empty: fail closed.\n */\npublic final class SessionService {\n\n    /** 32 bytes -> 43 unpadded base64url characters. */\n    private static final int TOKEN_BYTES = 32;\n    private static final int TOKEN_CHARS = 43;\n    private static final int REF_BYTES = 16;\n\n    private static final String INSERT_SQL = \"\"\"\n            INSERT INTO auth_session (token_hash, session_ref, account_id, created_at,\n                                      last_used_at, absolute_expires_at, revoked)\n            VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?, ?, 0)\n            \"\"\";\n\n    private static final String SELECT_SQL = \"\"\"\n            SELECT session_ref, account_id, last_used_at, absolute_expires_at, revoked\n            FROM auth_session WHERE token_hash = ?\n            \"\"\";\n\n    private static final String TOUCH_SQL = \"\"\"\n            UPDATE auth_session SET last_used_at = ? WHERE token_hash = ? AND revoked = 0\n            \"\"\";\n\n    private static final String REVOKE_SQL = \"\"\"\n            UPDATE auth_session SET revoked = 1 WHERE token_hash = ?\n            \"\"\";\n\n    private static final String REVOKE_ACCOUNT_SQL = \"\"\"\n            UPDATE auth_session SET revoked = 1 WHERE account_id = ?\n            \"\"\";\n\n    private static final String PRUNE_SQL = \"\"\"\n            DELETE FROM auth_session WHERE absolute_expires_at <= ? OR revoked = 1\n            \"\"\";\n\n    /**\n     * A freshly issued session. {@code token} is the only copy of the secret and is the caller's to\n     * wipe; {@code reference} is a non-secret handle safe to put in logs.\n     */\n    public record IssuedSession(String reference, char[] token, Instant expiresAt) {\n    }\n\n    private final Config config;\n    private final SecureRandom random = new SecureRandom();\n\n    public SessionService(Config config) {\n        this.config = config;\n    }\n\n    /** Creates a session for {@code accountId}. Call only after authentication has succeeded. */\n    public IssuedSession issue(Connection connection, long accountId, Instant now)\n            throws SQLException {\n        byte[] tokenBytes = new byte[TOKEN_BYTES];\n        random.nextBytes(tokenBytes);\n        byte[] refBytes = new byte[REF_BYTES];\n        random.nextBytes(refBytes);\n\n        char[] token = null;\n        try {\n            token = base64Url(tokenBytes);\n            String reference = Hex.encode(refBytes);\n            String tokenHash = hashToken(token);\n            Instant absoluteExpiry = now.plus(config.sessionAbsoluteTimeout());\n\n            try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(INSERT_SQL)) {\n                ps.setString(1, tokenHash);\n                ps.setString(2, reference);\n                ps.setLong(3, accountId);\n                ps.setLong(4, now.getEpochSecond());\n                ps.setLong(5, now.getEpochSecond());\n                ps.setLong(6, absoluteExpiry.getEpochSecond());\n                ps.executeUpdate();\n            }\n            prune(connection, now);\n\n            Instant idleExpiry = now.plus(config.sessionIdleTimeout());\n            Instant expiresAt = idleExpiry.isBefore(absoluteExpiry) ? idleExpiry : absoluteExpiry;\n            IssuedSession issued = new IssuedSession(reference, token, expiresAt);\n            token = null; // ownership transferred to the caller\n            return issued;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.bytes(tokenBytes);\n            Wipe.bytes(refBytes);\n            Wipe.chars(token);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** The account a valid token belongs to, or empty. Also refreshes the idle timer. */\n    public Optional<ValidSession> validate(Connection connection, char[] token, Instant now)\n            throws SQLException {\n        if (!isWellFormedToken(token)) {\n            return Optional.empty();\n        }\n        String tokenHash = hashToken(token);\n        String reference;\n        long accountId;\n        try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(SELECT_SQL)) {\n            ps.setString(1, tokenHash);\n            try (ResultSet rs = ps.executeQuery()) {\n                if (!rs.next()) {\n                    return Optional.empty();\n                }\n                if (rs.getInt(\"revoked\") != 0) {\n                    return Optional.empty();\n                }\n                long lastUsed = rs.getLong(\"last_used_at\");\n                long absoluteExpiry = rs.getLong(\"absolute_expires_at\");\n                long nowSeconds = now.getEpochSecond();\n                if (nowSeconds >= absoluteExpiry) {\n                    return Optional.empty();\n                }\n                if (nowSeconds - lastUsed >= config.sessionIdleTimeout().getSeconds()) {\n                    return Optional.empty();\n                }\n                reference = rs.getString(\"session_ref\");\n                accountId = rs.getLong(\"account_id\");\n            }\n        }\n        // Same transaction as the checks above (rule 7): the row cannot be revoked between the\n        // decision and the refresh.\n        try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(TOUCH_SQL)) {\n            ps.setLong(1, now.getEpochSecond());\n            ps.setString(2, tokenHash);\n            if (ps.executeUpdate() != 1) {\n                return Optional.empty();\n            }\n        }\n        return Optional.of(new ValidSession(reference, accountId));\n    }\n\n    /** A session that passed every server-side check. */\n    public record ValidSession(String reference, long accountId) {\n    }\n\n    /** Revokes one session. Succeeds silently for an unknown token: nothing to disclose. */\n    public void revoke(Connection connection, char[] token) throws SQLException {\n        if (!isWellFormedToken(token)) {\n            return;\n        }\n        try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(REVOKE_SQL)) {\n            ps.setString(1, hashToken(token));\n            ps.executeUpdate();\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Revokes every session of an account, e.g. after a password change. */\n    public void revokeAllForAccount(Connection connection, long accountId) throws SQLException {\n        try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(REVOKE_ACCOUNT_SQL)) {\n            ps.setLong(1, accountId);\n            ps.executeUpdate();\n        }\n    }\n\n    private void prune(Connection connection, Instant now) throws SQLException {\n        try (PreparedStatement ps = connection.prepareStatement(PRUNE_SQL)) {\n            ps.setLong(1, now.getEpochSecond());\n            ps.executeUpdate();\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Strict format check before the token is used for anything (rule 1 and rule 4): fixed length,\n     * base64url alphabet only. A value that cannot be one of our tokens never reaches a query.\n     */\n    static boolean isWellFormedToken(char[] token) {\n        if (token == null || token.length != TOKEN_CHARS) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (char c : token) {\n            boolean ok = (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') || (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z')\n                    || (c >= '0' && c <= '9') || c == '-' || c == '_';\n            if (!ok) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    private static String hashToken(char[] token) {\n        byte[] ascii = new byte[token.length];\n        try {\n            for (int i = 0; i < token.length; i++) {\n                ascii[i] = (byte) token[i]; // validated to be base64url ASCII\n            }\n            MessageDigest digest = MessageDigest.getInstance(\"SHA-256\");\n            return Hex.encode(digest.digest(ascii));\n        } catch (NoSuchAlgorithmException e) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"SHA-256 unavailable\", e);\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.bytes(ascii);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static char[] base64Url(byte[] bytes) {\n        byte[] encoded = Base64.getUrlEncoder().withoutPadding().encode(bytes);\n        try {\n            char[] out = new char[encoded.length];\n            for (int i = 0; i < encoded.length; i++) {\n                out[i] = (char) (encoded[i] & 0xff);\n            }\n            return out;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.bytes(encoded);\n        }\n java.util.logging.Logger;\nimport java.text.Normalizer;\n\n/**\n * Registration and authentication.\n *\n * <p>This is where the enumeration-resistance rules are enforced, so the ordering of operations\n * matters as much as the operations themselves:\n *\n * <ul>\n *   <li>Rule 6 (failure behaviour): {@link #register} returns the same {@link RegistrationStatus}\n *       whether the account was created or the email/username was already taken, and it hashes the\n *       password <em>before</em> attempting the insert in both cases, so neither the message nor\n *       the elapsed AccountService {\n\n    /** Result of a registration request. Carries no information about existing accounts. */\n    public enum RegistrationStatus {\n        /**\n         * The request was accepted. Returned both when a new account was created and when the\n         * email or username was already in use -- the caller cannot tell which, by design.\n         */\n        ACCEPTED,\n        /** Too many recent registration attempts from this source. */\n        THROTTLED\n    }\n\n    /** Result of an authentication attempt. */\n    public enum AuthStatus {\n        AUTHENTICATED,\n        /** Unknown identifier, wrong password, or disabled account -- indistinguishable. */\n        INVALID_CREDENTIALS,\n        /** Too many recent failures for this source or this identifier. */\n        THROTTLED\n    }\n\n    /** Authentication outcome; *\n * <p>Rule 3 (authentication and access): failures are counted <em>per source</em> and\n * <em>per account</em>. The per-source counter is what actually stops credential stuffing, because\n * it bites regardless of how many different accounts the attacker spreads across. The per-account\n * counter is the backstop for a distributed attack on one high-value account.\n *\n * <p>The per-account counter is deliberately a <em>short sliding window</em>, not a lock: it never\n * needs an administrator to clear it, it expires on its own after\n * {@link Config#throttleWindow()} (15 minutes by default) and any successful login clears it\n * immediately. Checks are allowlists -- anything not explicitly permitted is\n * rejected rather than stripped, escaped or \"fixed\", because silent repair is how two inputs end up\n * colliding on one account.\n *\n * <p>Rule 5: upper bounds exist on every field so a caller cannot make us store or hash something\n * arbitrarily large.\n */\npublic final class InputValidator {\n\n    public static final int EMAIL_MAX_LENGTH = 254;      // RFC 5321 path limit\n    public static final int EMAIL_LOCAL_MAX_LENGTH = 64;\n    public static final int USERNAME_MIN_LENGTH = 3;\n    public static final int USERNAME_MAX_LENGTH = 32;\n    public static final int PASSWORD_MIN_LENGTH = 12;    // OWASP minimum for human-chosen secrets\n    public static final int PASSWORD_MAX_LENGTH = 128;   // bounds hashing work; well above need\n\n    /** Local-part characters we accept. + \" characters.\");\n        }\n        return out;\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isDisallowedControl(char c) {\n        if (c == '\\t' || c == '\\n' || c == '\\r') {\n            return true;\n        }\n        int type = Character.getType(c);\n        return type == Character.CONTROL || type == Character.FORMAT\n                || type == Character.LINE_SEPARATOR || type == Character.PARAGRAPH_SEPARATOR\n                || type == Character.SURROGATE || type == Character.PRIVATE_USE\n                || type == Character.UNASSIGNED;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Enforces the password policy on an already-prepared password.\n     *\n     * @param prepared  output of {@link #preparePassword(char[])}\n     * @param canonicalUsername canonical username, or {@code null} to skip the similarity check\n     * @param canonicalEmail    canonical email, or {@code null} to skip the similarity check\n     */\n    public static void checkPasswordPolicy(char[] prepared, String canonicalUsername,\n                                           String canonicalEmail) throws ValidationException {\n        if (prepared == null || prepared.length < PASSWORD_MIN_LENGTH) {\n            throw new ValidationException(\"Password must be at least \" + PASSWORD_MIN_LENGTH + \" characters.\");\n        }\n        if (prepared.length > PASSWORD_MAX_LENGTH) {\n            throw new ValidationException(\"Password must be at most \" + PASSWORD_MAX_LENGTH + \" characters.\");\n        }\n        if (countDistinct(prepared) < 5) {\n            throw new ValidationException(\"Password must use at least 5 different characters.\");\n        }\n        // Folded copy for the comparisons below. Note that no String is built from the password:\n        // a String could not be wiped and would survive in the heap (rule 5).\n        char[] folded = new char[prepared.length];\n        for (int i = 0; i < prepared.length; i++) {\n            folded[i] = Character.toLowerCase(prepared[i]);\n        }\n        try {\n            for (String blocked : BLOCKED_PASSWORDS) {\n                if (equalsChars(folded, blocked)) {\n                    throw new ValidationException(\"That password is too common. Choose a different one.\");\n                }\n            }\n            if (canonicalUsername != null && canonicalUsername.length() >= 3\n                    && containsChars(folded, canonicalUsername)) {\n                throw new ValidationException(\"Password must not contain your username.\");\n            }\n            if (canonicalEmail != null) {\n                int at = canonicalEmail.indexOf('@');\n                String localPart = at > 0 ? canonicalEmail.substring(0, at) : canonicalEmail;\n                if (localPart.length() >= 3 && containsChars(folded, localPart)) {\n                    throw new ValidationException(\"Password must not contain your email address.\");\n                }\n            }\n            if (isSingleRepeatedCharacter(folded) || isMonotonicRun(folded)) {\n                throw new ValidationException(\"That password is too predictable. Choose a different one.\");\n            }\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(folded);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static int countDistinct(char[] value) {\n        int distinct = 0;\n        outer:\n        for (int i = 0; i < value.length; i++) {\n            for (int j = 0; j < i; j++) {\n                if (value[j] == value[i]) {\n                    continue outer;\n                }\n            }\n            distinct++;\n        }\n        return distinct;\n    }\n\n    private static boolean equalsChars(char[] value, String other) {\n        if (value.length != other.length()) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (int i = 0; i < value.length; i++) {\n            if (value[i] != other.charAt(i)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    private static boolean containsChars(char[] haystack, String needle) {\n        int n = needle.length();\n        if (n == 0 || n > haystack.length) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (int i = 0; Please try again later.\");\n            exitCode = 1;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.bytes(config.pepper());\n        }\n        System.exit(exitCode);\n    }\n\n    private static int register(AccountService service, Logger log) {\n        char[] password = null;\n        char[] confirmation = null;\n        try {\n            String email = prompt(\"Email address: \");\n            String username = prompt(\"Username: \");\n            password = readSecret(\"Password: \");\n            confirmation = readSecret(\"Confirm password: \");\n            if (password == null || confirmation == null) {\n                System.err.println(\"No password provided.\");\n                return 1;\n            }\n            if (!Arrays.equals(password, confirmation)) {\n                System.err.println(\"The two passwords do not match.\");\n                return 1;\n            }\n            AccountService.RegistrationStatus status =\n                    service.register(email, username, password, sourceId());\n            // Identical wording for \"created\" and \"already taken\" (rule 6).\n            System.out.println(switch (status) {\n                case ACCEPTED -> \"Registration request accepted. Please try again later.\");\n            return 1;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(password);\n            Wipe.chars(confirmation);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static int login(AccountService service, Config config, Logger log) {\n        char[] password = null;\n        AccountService.AuthResult result = null;\n        try {\n            String identifier = prompt(\"Email or username: \");\n            password = readSecret(\"Password: \");\n            if (password == null) {\n                System.err.println(\"No password provided.\");\n                return 1;\n            }\n            result = service.authenticate(identifier, password, sourceId());\n            switch (result.status()) {\n                case AUTHENTICATED -> {\n                    SessionService.IssuedSession session = result.session();\n                    storeSessionToken(config, session.token());\n                    System.out.println(\"Signed in as \" + result.usernameDisplay() + \".\");\n                    System.out.println(\"Session \" + session.reference() + \" expires at \"\n                            + TS.format(session.expiresAt()) + \".\");\n                    return 0;\n                }\n                case THROTTLED -> {\n                    System.err.println(\"Too many attempts. Please try again later.\");\n                    return 1;\n                }\n                default -> {\n                    // One message for unknown account, wrong password and disabled account.\n                    System.err.println(\"Invalid credentials.\");\n                    return 1;\n                }\n            }\n        } catch (Exception e) {\n            log.log(Level.SEVERE, \"login failed\", e);\n            System.err.println(\"Something went wrong. Please try again later.\");\n            return 1;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(password);\n            if (result != null && result.session() != null) {\n                Wipe.chars(result.session().token());\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static int whoami(AccountService service, Config config, Logger log) {\n        char[] token = null;\n        try {\n            token = readSessionToken(config);\n            if (token == null) {\n                System.err.println(\"Not signed in.\");\n                return 1;\n            }\n            Optional<SessionService.ValidSession> session = service.resolveSession(token);\n            if (session.isEmpty()) {\n                // Expired, revoked, unknown or malformed all look the same: fail closed.\n                System.err.println(\"Not signed in.\");\n                return 1;\n            }\n            System.out.println(\"Signed in with session \" + session.get().reference() + \".\");\n            return 0;\n        } catch (Exception e) {\n            log.log(Level.SEVERE, \"session lookup failed\", e);\n            System.err.println(\"Something went wrong. Please try again later.\");\n            return 1;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(token);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static int logout(AccountService service, Config config, Logger log) {\n        char[] token = null;\n        try {\n            token = readSessionToken(config);\n            if (token != null) {\n                service.logout(token);\n            }\n            deleteSessionFile(config, log);\n            // Same message whether or not there was a session to revoke.\n            System.out.println(\"Signed out.\");\n            return 0;\n        } catch (Exception e) {\n            log.log(Level.SEVERE, \"logout failed\", e);\n            System.err.println(\"Something went wrong. Please try again later.\");\n            return 1;\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(token);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * The request origin as observed by this process. For the CLI that is the local OS account; an\n     * HTTP front end must pass the socket peer address instead, never a client-supplied header.\n     */\n    private static String sourceId() {\n        String user = System.getProperty(\"user.name\");\n        return \"cli:\" + (user == null || user.isBlank() ? \"unknown\" : user);\n    }\n\n    private static synchronized Reader stdin() {\n        if (stdin == null) {\n            stdin = new InputStreamReader(System.in, StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n        }\n        return stdin;\n    }\n\n    /** Reads a non-secret line. Bounded, so a huge paste cannot be used to exhaust memory. */\n    private static String prompt(String label) throws IOException {\n        if (System.console() != null) {\n            String line = System.console().readLine(\"%s\", label);\n            return line == null ? \"\" : line;\n        }\n        System.out.print(label);\n        System.out.flush();\n        char[] line = readLine(stdin(), InputValidator.EMAIL_MAX_LENGTH);\n        if (line == null) {\n            return \"\";\n        }\n        try {\n            return new String(line);\n        } finally {\n            Wipe.chars(line);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Reads one line of at most {@code maxLength} characters into a wipeable array, draining any\n     * overflow so the next prompt is not fed our leftovers. Returns {@code null} on an empty line.\n     */\n    private static char[] readLine(Reader reader, int maxLength) throws IOException {\n        char[] buffer = new char[maxLength + 1];\n        int length = 0;\n        while (true) {\n            int c = reader.read();\n            if (c < 0 || c == '\\n') {\n                break;\n            }\n            if (c == '\\r') {\n                continue;\n            }\n            if (length == buffer.length) {\n                while (c >= 0 && c != '\\n') {\n                    c = reader.read();\n                }\n                break;\n            }\n            buffer[length++] = (char) c;\n        }\n        if (length == 0) {\n            Wipe.chars(buffer);\n            return null;\n        }\n        char[] line = Arrays.copyOf(buffer, length);\n        Wipe.chars(buffer);\n        return line;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Reads a secret without echoing it. Falls back to standard input only when explicitly enabled,\n     * and never to a command-line argument.\n     */\n    private static char[] readSecret(String label) throws IOException {\n        if (System.console() != null) {\n            return System.console().readPassword(\"%s\", label);\n        }\n        if (!\"true\".equalsIgnoreCase(System.getenv(\"ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN\"))) {\n            throw new IOException(\"no terminal available for password entry; \"\n                    + \"set ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN=true to read it from standard input\");\n        }\n        // Read into a char[] rather than a String, which could not be wiped afterwards. One extra\n        // character of headroom so an over-long password is seen and rejected, not truncated.\n        return readLine(stdin(), InputValidator.PASSWORD_MAX_LENGTH + 1);\n    }\n\n Passwords are stored as **Argon2id** hashes in PHC\nstring format; successful authentication mints a server-side session.\n\n```\nsrc/main/java/com/example/accounts/\n  App.java                CLI: register / login / whoami / logout\n  Config.java             validated configuration from the environment\n  Database.java           connection factory, schema, atomic transaction helper\n  AccountService.java     registration + authentication orchestration\n  AccountRepository.java  all SQL for the account table (prepared statements only)\n  PasswordHasher.java     Argon2id hashing, PHC encoding, strict parsing, constant-time compare\n  InputValidator.java     allowlist validation + canonicalisation of email/username/password\n  ThrottleService.java    per-source and per-account attempt limiting\n  SessionService.java     CSPRNG session tokens, stored as digests, server-side expiry\n  SessionCookie.java      Set-Cookie builder for an HTTP front end\n  Logging.java, Mask.java, Wipe.java, Hex.java, ValidationException.java\n```\n\n## Build and run\n\n```bash\nmvn package                      # runs the 91 tests, then builds target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar register\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar login\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar whoami\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar logout\n```\n\nThe password is read from the terminal with echo off. There is no `--password` flag: command-line\narguments are world-readable via `/proc` and persist in shell history. For automation, set\n`ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN=true` and pipe the prompts' answers on standard input.\n\n`login` never prints the session token. It writes it to `$ACCOUNTS_DATA_DIR/session.token` mode\n`0600` and prints only the non-secret session reference, which is the same handle that appears in\nthe log.\n\n### Configuration\n\nEvery variable is validated; an unparsable or out-of-range value aborts startup rather than falling\nback to something weaker.\n\n| Variable | Default | Accepted |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `ACCOUNTS_DATA_DIR` | `~/.local/share/secure-accounts` | absolute path, created `0700` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_JDBC_URL` | `jdbc:sqlite:<data dir>/accounts.db` | must start with `jdbc:sqlite:` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_PEPPER_B64` | unset | base64 decoding to 16–64 bytes |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_MEMORY_KIB` | `65536` | 19456–1048576 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_ITERATIONS` | `3` | 2–10 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_PARALLELISM` | `1` | 1–8 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_MAX_FAILURES_PER_ACCOUNT` | `10` | 3–100 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_MAX_FAILURES_PER_SOURCE` | `25` | 5–1000 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_THROTTLE_WINDOW_SECONDS` | `900` | 60–86400 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_SESSION_IDLE_SECONDS` | `1800` | 60–86400 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_SESSION_ABSOLUTE_SECONDS` | `43200` | ≥ idle, ≤ 604800 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_LOG_TO_FILE` | `true` | `true/false/1/0/yes/no` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN` | unset | `true` to allow non-terminal secret entry |\n\n`ACCOUNTS_PEPPER_B64` is an optional site-wide secret mixed into Argon2 as its `secret` parameter.\nWith it set, a stolen database alone is not enough to mount an offline guessing attack. Generate one\nwith `head -c 32 /dev/urandom | base64` and deliver it the way you deliver other service secrets\n(systemd `LoadCredential`, a secrets manager) — not in a shell profile, and never on a command line.\nChanging it invalidates existing hashes.\n\n## How passwords are stored\n\n```\n$argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=1$<16-byte salt, b64>$<32-byte tag, b64>\n```\n\nArgon2id with `m=64 MiB, t=3, p=1` and a fresh 16-byte `SecureRandom` salt per password — at or\nabove the current OWASP floor (`m=19 MiB, t=2, p=1`). The standard PHC encoding means the cost\nparameters travel with the hash, so they can be raised later without a migration: on a successful\nlogin, `PasswordHasher.needsRehash` notices an outdated hash and `AccountService` rewrites it from\nthe plaintext it already has in hand. A stored hash is treated as untrusted input on the way back in\n— a tampered `m=67108864` row is rejected, not allocated.\n\n## Rules this feature was written against\n\n### 1. Trust boundaries\n\nInputs this code can receive: the email, username and password typed by the user; the login\nidentifier; the session token (from the token file, or from a cookie in an HTTP deployment); the\nrequest source identifier; every `ACCOUNTS_*` environment variable; and every value read back out of\nthe database (password hash, display name, timestamps, counters).\n\n* `InputValidator` checks each registration field for type, length, allowed characters and range\n  *before* it reaches the database, the hasher or a log line, and does so with **allowlists**:\n  ASCII-only email in a restricted dot-atom subset (≤254 chars, local ≤64, domain labels ≤63,\n  alphabetic TLD); usernames NFKC-folded then limited to `[a-z0-9._-]`, 3–32 chars, alnum at both\n  ends, no doubled separators, reserved names refused; passwords 12–128 characters with control and\n  format characters rejected.\n* NFKC folding before the uniqueness check is what prevents a homoglyph twin: full-width `Ａdmin`\n  folds to the reserved `admin`, and `ａｌｉｃｅ９９` cannot become a second \"alice99\".\n* All SQL goes through `PreparedStatement` with bound parameters — no user value is ever\n  concatenated into a statement. `sqlMetacharactersInInputAreJustCharacters` asserts that\n  `a'; DROP TABLE account;--` is simply an invalid identifier.\n* Values read *out* of the database are validated too: `PasswordHasher.parse` enforces algorithm,\n  version, parameter ranges and salt/tag lengths, and `SessionService.validate` rejects anything\n  that is not exactly 43 base64url characters before it reaches a query.\n* Configuration is range-checked in `Config`; nothing a hostile environment can set turns into a\n  weaker security posture, only into a refusal to start.\n* No security outcome is decided by a client-controlled value. Authentication is decided by the\n  stored hash, session validity by the `auth_session` row, and rate limiting by the `auth_attempt`\n  rows — all server-side state.\n\n### 2. Secrets\n\n* There are no compiled-in credentials, keys or passwords. The program runs with no secret at all;\n  the optional pepper comes from the environment.\n* The pepper is never printed, logged, persisted beside the hash, or passed as an argument.\n  `Config.toString()` reports only `pepperConfigured=true|false`; its decoding failure message\n  deliberately does not echo the value; `App` wipes the byte array on exit; and\n  `doesNotLeakThePepperIntoTheEncodedHash` asserts it does not appear in stored output.\n* Passwords are never logged at any level, never echoed, and never accepted as a command-line\n  argument. `Mask` provides helpers for emails and usernames and deliberately provides none for\n  passwords or tokens, so there is no \"safe rendering\" of them to reach for.\n* The data directory is `0700` and the database, log and session-token files are `0600`. Nothing\n  sensitive is written anywhere another local user can read.\n\n### 3. Authentication and access\n\n* A password is only ever accepted by `PasswordHasher.verify` against the hash stored for that\n  account. There is no other path to `AuthStatus.AUTHENTICATED`.\n* Everything that is not a positive verification fails closed: unknown identifier, wrong password,\n  disabled account, corrupt stored hash, unparsable cost parameters, unknown/expired/revoked\n  session, malformed token, zero rows updated on the session refresh. `aCorruptStoredHashFailsClosed`\n  and `aDisabledAccountCannotLogIn…` cover two of those.\n* Attempts are limited **per source and per account** (`ThrottleService`). The per-source limit is\n  what actually stops credential stuffing, because it bites however many accounts the attacker\n  spreads across; the per-account limit is the backstop against a distributed attack on one\n  high-value account.\n* **The per-account limit cannot be used to lock a legitimate user out.** It is a 15-minute sliding\n  window, not a lock: it expires by itself with no administrator involved\n  (`throttlesRepeatedFailuresForOneAccount` advances the clock and logs in), and the real user's own\n  successful login clears it immediately (`aSuccessfulLoginClearsTheAccountCounter`). Registration\n  is limited per *source only*, so an attacker cannot drain a victim's account budget by repeatedly\n  \"registering\" their address (`anAttackerCannotBurnTheAccountCounterThroughRegistration`).\n* Conversely, a successful login does **not** clear the source counter — otherwise an attacker\n  holding one valid credential could launder their own budget between probes\n  (`aSuccessfulLoginDoesNotResetTheSourceBudget`).\n* A blocked attempt is not counted, so a blocked source stops feeding the account counter and that\n  counter ages out. The throttle gate also runs *before* the Argon2 work, so a blocked caller cannot\n  make the server spend 64 MiB and three passes per request.\n\n### 4. Identifiers and tokens\n\n* Session tokens are 32 bytes from `SecureRandom`, generated by `SessionService.issue` — never\n  accepted from, chosen by, or derivable from the caller, and not a function of the account or the\n  time. A caller-supplied value of the right shape resolves to nothing\n  (`resolvesAValidSessionAndRejectsEverythingElse`), so there is no session fixation.\n* Only `SHA-256(token)` is stored, so reading the database does not let you replay a session. The\n  separate 16-byte `session_ref` is the non-secret handle used in logs and console output.\n* `SessionCookie` emits `__Host-session=<token>; Path=/; Max-Age=…; HttpOnly; Secure;\n  SameSite=Strict` — and no `Domain`. The `__Host-` prefix makes the browser itself enforce\n  Secure + `Path=/` + no Domain, so a sibling subdomain cannot plant the cookie over plain HTTP. The\n  cookie's own lifetime is never trusted: idle and absolute expiry are re-checked against the\n  database on every request.\n* This feature uses no externally provided identifier. The one externally influenced value is the\n  **source identifier** used for rate limiting. It must be server-observed — the socket peer address\n  for an HTTP front end, the local OS account for the CLI. If you ever source it from\n  `X-Forwarded-For`, only do so behind a proxy that is the sole possible writer of that header;\n  otherwise an attacker rotates the header and the per-source limit is worthless. `normalizeSourceId`\n  bounds and sanitises it, and a missing value falls into one shared bucket rather than escaping\n  rate limiting.\n\n### 5. Sensitive data\n\n* Logs carry `a***@example.com` and `a***(7)`, never a full address, never a password, never a\n  token. Log records are forced onto one line so an injected newline cannot forge an entry.\n* The log file is created *before* the handler opens it so its mode can be set to `0600`, and\n  rotation count is deliberately 1 — a higher count would have `FileHandler` create its own\n  `accounts.log.N` files under the process umask, i.e. world-readable.\n* Every stored text column is length-bounded by a `CHECK` constraint; the log is capped at 1 MiB;\n  the attempt table is pruned of expired windows on every write; the session table drops expired and\n  revoked rows; the session-token file is not read if it exceeds 128 bytes; console input is read\n  into fixed-size buffers with the overflow drained.\n* Credentials live in `char[]`/`byte[]` and are zeroed in `finally` blocks (`Wipe`). The password is\n  UTF-8 encoded through a `CharBuffer` so no unwipeable `String` copy is made, and the password\n  policy checks run on char arrays rather than on `new String(password)`.\n* Two residual exposures, accepted and noted rather than hidden: a password that is not already\n  NFC-normalised takes a path through `java.text.Normalizer`, which only returns `String` (rare, and\n  hand-rolling NFC would be worse); and `SessionCookie.setCookieHeader` necessarily returns a\n  `String` containing the token, which the HTTP layer should write to the response and drop.\n\n### 6. Failure behaviour\n\n* Users get generic errors — \"Invalid credentials.\", \"Too many attempts. Please try again later.\",\n  \"Something went wrong. Please try again later.\" Reasons, stack traces and the duplicate/new\n  distinction go to the `0600` log only.\n* **No response reveals whether an account exists.** Registration returns the identical\n  `ACCEPTED` status and identical wording whether the account was created or the email or username\n  was already taken, and hashes the password on both paths before the insert so the elapsed time\n  matches (`duplicateRegistrationIsIndistinguishableFromSuccess`). Login returns one\n  `INVALID_CREDENTIALS` for unknown identifier, wrong password, disabled account and corrupt hash,\n  and runs a full Argon2 verification against a decoy hash with the current parameters when there is\n  no account to check, so the work done is comparable\n  (`unknownAccountAndWrongPasswordAreIndistinguishable`). A password too malformed to prepare still\n  burns one decoy verification. Both throttle scopes produce the same message, so which limit fired\n  is not observable either. A reserved username is refused with the same \"not available\" wording a\n  taken one would get.\n* Invalid input is rejected, never coerced: an unknown CLI command exits 2 rather than guessing, an\n  out-of-range configuration value aborts startup rather than snapping to a default, a non-SQLite\n  JDBC URL is refused rather than attempted without its migration, and a stored hash with\n  unsupported parameters fails verification rather than being honoured.\n* The residual, unavoidable signal is the throttle status itself: being told \"too many attempts\"\n  reveals that *this source* has been failing, which is the point of telling the user.\n\n### 7. Concurrency\n\n* `Database.inTransaction` opens each unit of work with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` (via sqlite-jdbc's\n  `transaction_mode` pragma), taking the write lock up front, with `busy_timeout=5000` and WAL.\n* `ThrottleService.tryAcquire` is the whole check-then-act in one call inside that transaction:\n  read the counters, decide, increment. Two concurrent logins cannot both observe \"one attempt left\"\n  and both proceed.\n* Account uniqueness is **not** a read followed by a write. `AccountRepository.insert` relies on the\n  `UNIQUE` constraints and reports the constraint violation as \"taken\", so one of two simultaneous\n  registrations for the same address loses deterministically.\n  `concurrentRegistrationsForOneEmailCreateExactlyOneAccount` races eight threads and asserts one\n  row.\n* `SessionService.validate` checks revocation and both expiry timers and refreshes the idle timer in\n  the same transaction, so a session cannot be revoked between the decision and the refresh; the\n  refresh is itself guarded by `WHERE revoked = 0` and a row-count check.\n* Session minting and the account-counter clear happen together in one transaction.\n\n### 8. Prefer the platform\n\n* Argon2id comes from Bouncy Castle's reviewed implementation; randomness from `SecureRandom`;\n  digest comparison from `MessageDigest.isEqual` (constant-time); base64 from `java.util.Base64`;\n  Unicode folding from `java.text.Normalizer`; password entry from `System.console().readPassword`;\n  file modes from `java.nio.file.PosixFilePermissions`; atomicity and uniqueness from the database\n  engine. The on-disk format is the standard PHC string, so other tools can verify these hashes.\n* Nothing cryptographic is hand-rolled. The one algorithm not in the JDK is Argon2 itself, which is\n  the sole reason a third-party dependency appears at all.\n\n#### Dependency provenance\n\nPinned to exact versions from Maven Central over HTTPS, with `checksumPolicy=fail` and snapshots\ndisabled. No mirrors, no snapshot repositories, nothing loaded from a shared or world-writable\nlocation, and no code fetched at runtime. Verified SHA-256 of the artifacts this was built and\ntested against:\n\n| Artifact | SHA-256 |\n| --- | --- |\n| `org.bouncycastle:bcprov-jdk18on:1.79` | `0d81ecc3124536b539bce9aa3fe9621b7f84c9cee371b635a5b31c78b79ab1da` |\n| `org.xerial:sqlite-jdbc:3.47.1.0` | `4164d95347aeab42b754cabb7945b93e54f29ddb2eded967dbe979b4045cb685` |\n| `org.slf4j:slf4j-nop:2.0.16` | `deca6c04ed35515a0a911fa44c0e836bee92c0c59d2e8fa9bab8ffbc464a9ba7` |\n\n`slf4j-nop` is present only to bind sqlite-jdbc's logging to a no-op so the driver cannot write\nanywhere unexpected. Check these before trusting a build:\n`sha256sum ~/.m2/repository/org/bouncycastle/bcprov-jdk18on/1.79/bcprov-jdk18on-1.79.jar`.\n\n## Deliberate scope limits\n\nThese are design decisions, not oversights.\n\n* **No email verification.** A real deployment must confirm ownership of the address before the\n  account can do anything, and must keep the same enumeration-resistant wording when it does\n  (\"if that address was available, check your inbox\").\n* **No password reset or change.** `SessionService.revokeAllForAccount` is the intended hook: a\n  password change must revoke every existing session.\n* **Breached-password blocklist is a stub.** `InputValidator.BLOCKED_PASSWORDS` holds twenty common\n  values. Wire it to a full list (e.g. a local Pwned Passwords SHA-1 range file, queried offline —\n  not a live third-party API call carrying user passwords) before production.\n* **The email local part is case-folded.** Strictly, `Alice@` and `alice@` are different mailboxes\n  per RFC 5321. Folding is a deliberate product choice so users cannot create two accounts they\n  cannot tell apart; it rules out the rare provider that is local-part case-sensitive.\n* **The email allowlist is narrower than RFC 5322.** Quoted local parts, the rarer RFC\n  punctuation (`!#$%&'*/=?^_` and friends) and internationalised (IDN/UTF-8) addresses are refused. Widening it means widening the allowlist\n  explicitly, with tests.\n* **SQLite only.** The schema is SQLite DDL. Another engine needs its own migration and its own\n  `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` equivalent; `Config` refuses other JDBC URLs rather than pretending.\n* **No HTTP layer.** `SessionCookie` is the contract for one. Such a layer must pass the socket peer\n  address as the source identifier, add CSRF defences appropriate to its endpoints (`SameSite=Strict`\n  covers a lot but not everything), and terminate TLS — `Secure` cookies are useless over plain HTTP.\n\n## Tests\n\n`mvn test` — 91 tests. Beyond the happy paths they assert the security properties above: that the\nplaintext password appears nowhere under the data directory, that duplicate registration is\nindistinguishable from success, that unknown-account and wrong-password are indistinguishable, that\nSQL metacharacters are inert, that a tampered hash row with hostile cost parameters is refused, that\neight concurrent registrations produce one account, that both throttle scopes fire and that neither\ncan be turned into a lockout, that tokens are unpredictable and stored only as digests, that\nrevocation and both expiry timers work, that the attempt table holds only digests, and that the data\ndirectory and database are owner-only.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"e5b2cdb7-d5e7-4c25-a642-af8b2e9f5138","session_id":"d3e1b2da-be63-4eb3-834e-c037285a88c3","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"e5b2cdb7-d5e7-4c25-a642-af8b2e9f5138","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"This is a very thorough README. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, location, and remediation\n- [ ] False positive rate is below 10%\n########## exploiting-race-condition-vulnerabilities\n- single-packet-attack\n- limit-overrun\n- burp-suite\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1505.003\n- T1083\n- T1027\n---\n\n# Exploiting Race Condition Vulnerabilities\n\n## When to Use\n- When testing applications with transaction-based functionality (payments, transfers, coupons)\n- During assessment of rate-limiting or attempt-limiting mechanisms\n- When testing multi-step workflows (registration, password reset, MFA)\n- During bug bounty hunting for logic flaws in state-changing operations\n- When evaluating applications with inventory or balance management systems\n\n## Prerequisites\n- Burp Suite Professional with Turbo Intruder extension installed\n- Understanding of HTTP/2 single-packet attack technique\n- Python I have everything needed. Let me finalize the findings, severity ordering, and write the report.\n\nFinal findings list (severity ordered):\n\n**SEC-01 — High: Authentication completes across two separate transactions; the account row read in tx1 is used to mint a session and overwrite the password hash in tx2 without re-checking state (TOCTOU / lost update).**\n- Location: `AccountService.java:156-212` (main evidence 176, 189-190, 202-208)\n- CWE-367 (Time-of-check Time-of-use Race Condition) — also CWE-863 incorrect authorization.\n- Why: `disabled()` is evaluated at line 176 against the row loaded in tx1; between that transaction committing and tx2 opening, an administrator disabling the account (or a concurrent rehash) is not observed: tx2 issues a session for `accountId` regardless, and `updatePasswordHash` blindly overwrites the hash with `upgraded` derived from the hash read before. With two concurrent logins using two different *old* passwords (possible while a password change is in flight — or with a password-change feature added later), the last writer wins and resurrects a stale hash. The `README` rule 3/7 claim about check-and-count atomicity holds for the throttle but not for the account state that authorizes the session.\n- Actually careful: there's no password change feature in scope. So the strongest concrete failure is: session minted for an account disabled between tx1 and tx2 → the disabled-account control is checked on stale state, and the write in tx2 is unconditional (no `WHERE password_hash = <previous value>` guard), so a lost update is possible. Let me phrase the failure scenario precisely and honestly, and note the second-order risk (stale-hash resurrection when a reset/change flow is added — README says deliberately out of scope, but the pattern is there).\n\nSeverity: High? It's a narrow window (SQLite, IMMEDIATE transactions, single-process CLI). Consequence: access control bypass window (session for disabled account) + lost update on credential material. I'd rate **High** for the correctness of an authorization decision on stale data — but honestly with SQLite BEGIN IMMEDIATE and this being a CLI... Window is milliseconds. I'll call it **High** because the failure is \"certifies-falsely\" on an access-control decision and the fix is cheap, but I should be measured. Let me rate Medium-High... The format wants Severity: Critical/High/Medium/Low presumably. I'll use High with an explicit note about window size and exploitability conditions. Hmm — overclaiming is worse. Let me think about what's genuinely the worst.\n\nActually there's a cleaner, more provable statement: **`throttle.clearAccount()` runs unconditionally on the account derived from a *previous* transaction, and more importantly the whole authenticate flow gives an attacker unlimited attempts to disable-check...** no.\n\nLet me reconsider the strongest genuinely-High findings:\n\n1. **Unsalted SHA-256 throttle keys when pepper is unset (the default)** → `auth_attempt` table stores `SHA256(\"source:\" + clientIp)` and `SHA256(\"account:\" + email)`. IP space is 2^32 → brute-forceable in seconds; emails are trivially recoverable with a wordlist/rules. So the table is effectively a plaintext log of client IPs and account identifiers, contradicting the documented claim (rule 5) that \"only a digest is kept so the table is not a credential/PII store.\" An attacker who steals the DB file (the same threat model as the password hashes — and the whole reason Argon2 + hashed session tokens exist) recovers the full authentication-attempt log: which IPs attempted which accounts, and failure counts. That's a real sensitive-data-at-rest exposure with a small key space. CWE-916 is about password-based system access; better fit: **CWE-261: Weak Encoding for Password** — hmm, it's not a password. Better: **CWE-326 Inadequate Encryption Strength**? The cleanest: **CWE-916** no. I'd use **CWE-261**... Let me instead use **CWE-359**? no.\n\nThe standard mapping for \"unsalted hash of low-entropy data\" is CWE-916 (\"Use of Password Hash With Insufficient Computational Effort\") when it's passwords. For identifiers/IPs it's better expressed as **CWE-312 Cleartext Storage of Sensitive Information** (functionally: the digest is recoverable, so it's equivalent to storing them in the clear) with a note of CWE-916/CWE-760 family (use of a one-way hash with a small input space, no salt). I'll cite **CWE-312** primary and mention CWE-916-style unsalted-hash weakness. Actually CWE-760 \"Use of a One-Way Hash with a Predictable Input\" is a very precise fit! CWE-760: Use of a One-Way Hash with a Predictable Input. Yes — that's exact. Primary: CWE-760, secondary CWE-312.\n\nSeverity: **High**? Consequence is disclosure of IP+account-attempt history from a stolen DB, i.e., the exact scenario the design tries to protect against. It's a default-configuration issue. I'd say **Medium** to **High**. The DB also contains password hashes and it's protected by file perms; the added harm is PII/telemetry recovery. Also: recoverable identifiers enable an attacker with DB write access... Not privilege escalation. I'll rate **Medium** and explain. Hmm, but it *is* the documented control (\"rule 5\") being false. Severity should reflect impact not doc-accuracy. Medium.\n\nWait — one more angle: the throttle key for scope=account is `SHA256(\"account:\" + canonicalIdentifier)`. An attacker who can insert rows (or who has DB read) can compute keys for target emails to *predict* other rows... not much.\n\nBut there is another consequence: because the key is deterministic and unsalted, an attacker who obtains a DB snapshot can *precompute* keys to plant rows that block specific victims (permanent account lockout — though `prune` ages them; window_start must be recent, so a planted row with a future window_start would block until... `failures()` returns failures if window_start > cutoff; a planted far-future window_start would block the account forever!). But planting requires DB write, which is already game over. And throttle is not a *security* control against DoS of legitimate users... it is availability though. Keep as availability note under a Low finding maybe. Actually there's a genuine non-planted lockout issue: per-source counter never cleared on success + source = shared NAT/`cli:<username>`. Let me keep that Low.\n\n2. **Argon2 work is skipped for unparsable stored hashes and for `usable == false` the decoy uses current params, not stored params** → timing/enumeration oracle. Precisely: `verify()` returns false at line 89/95 *without* deriving, so a login for an existing account whose stored hash is corrupt/out-of-policy is measurably faster than a valid-hash login. Also, when `usable==false` (account disabled) the code verifies against `decoyHash` (current params) while an enabled account verifies against its own stored params — if any account's stored params are weaker than current (the `needsRehash` path proves that's expected), a disabled-account attempt takes a *different* time than an enabled-account attempt with the same wrong password. That leaks (a) account-exists vs not in the corrupt case, (b) disabled vs enabled in the rehash-pending case. This directly undermines rule 6 / the `burnComparableWork` design intent. CWE-208: Observable Timing Discrepancy. Severity: **Medium**.\n\nAlso `burnComparableWork()` is called for unusable-password inputs, using the decoy hash — so a control-char password gets decoy timing, which differs from a real account's timing → also an existence oracle. Same finding.\n\n3. **Weak password policy: 20-entry blocklist stub + `countDistinct < 5`** → OWASP A07/ASVS, CWE-521. Concrete: `\"correct horse battery staple\"` fine, but `password123456` blocked, while `passw0rd123456` allowed; `1234567890ab` allowed? `isMonotonicRun` — need to check it catches digits only. Let me not overclaim specifics; the core is: no breached-password corpus, so any of the top-100M leaked passwords not in a 20-item list is accepted (e.g. `qwerty12345678`, `Zxcvbn!2024`), and distinct-char count of 5 lets `aaaaaaaaab` (10 chars? needs 12) — 12 chars with 5 distinct e.g. `aaaaaaaabbbb` passes; `isSingleRepeatedCharacter` only rejects all-same. So `aaaaaaaa-aaaa`... Let's check `isMonotonicRun` semantics quickly — I read it earlier as rejecting monotonic runs. `aabbccddeeff` has 6 distinct, not single-repeated, not monotonic → accepted. That's a dictionary-free pass. Fine, I'll state: 12-char minimum with only a 20-string denylist and a 5-distinct-character floor admits high-frequency leaked passwords and simple alternating patterns; this is the *only* line of defense before an offline attack if the DB leaks, and it's the credential-stuffing surface for online attack (throttle is 10/account per 15 min, but distributed sources defeat per-source).\n\nCWE-521 Weak Password Requirements. Severity: **Medium**.\n\n4. **DB file (and WAL siblings) created under the process umask, permissions tightened only afterwards, and only on files that already exist** → world-readable window; CWE-363 (Race Condition for Access Unsuccessful / symlink) + CWE-276. The `-journal`/`-wal` files are created by SQLite on first write *after* `restrictDatabaseFilePermissions` may already have run for a fresh DB; WAL is created lazily when the first connection switches to WAL, and subsequent checkpoints create new `-shm`/`-wal` — the chmod runs once, at startup only. So `accounts.db-wal` created later (or after a crash where SQLite recreates it) will be umask-default (0644 under typical umask 022), containing recent plaintext rows — including password hashes and session token hashes for logins since last checkpoint. That's a concrete, real exposure. Also the primary file exists at chmod time on first run (created by migrate), so it's covered — but only after a window. And `pathPart` handling: `if (pathPart.isEmpty() || pathPart.startsWith(\":\")) return;` — note `jdbc:sqlite::memory:` handled; but relative paths ok.\n\nAlso the check `Files.exists(p)` skips missing siblings → they never get tightened. Good, that's the crux.\n\nPrimary CWE: **CWE-276 Incorrect Default Permissions** (with CWE-363 TOCTOU element). Severity: **High** — plaintext hashes/token hashes readable by any local user under default umask, and the mitigation silently no-ops for files created later. It's local access required, so Medium-High. I'll say High because the failure is silent and covers WAL (which contains the *newest* hashes).\n\nHmm, but also note: `restrictDatabaseFilePermissions` catches IOException and only logs WARNING → fail-open. Reinforces.\n\n5. **`Config.pepper()` returns the live secret array; `PasswordHasher`/`ThrottleService` clone it and never wipe the clones; `App` wipes only the original in `finally`** → pepper copies persist in the heap for the JVM lifetime, defeating the wipe intent; plus pepper is optional so the default deployment has zero pepper. CWE-244 (Improper Management of Sensitive Data) / CWE-1173? The clean: **CWE-244: Improper Management of Sensitive Data** or CWE-798? not hardcoded. Use CWE-244. Severity: **Low-Medium**. I'd fold the \"optional pepper\" into the same finding? They're distinct: one is key-management hygiene, the other is a config default. I'll make pepper-optional part of SEC on unsalted keys? No — the pepper for hashing being optional is fine (documented, defense-in-depth). Keep SEC on live array + un-wiped clones, severity **Low**, since exploitation requires heap dump/core-file access which is already privileged.\n\nActually a more interesting and defensible variant: `Config.equals/hashCode` ignore pepper — not a vuln. `toString` masks. Fine.\n\n6. **`jdbc:sqlite:` allowlist is prefix-only, so `ACCOUNTS_JDBC_URL` (or `ACCOUNTS_DATA_DIR`) lets the process create/overwrite an arbitrary on-disk SQLite file anywhere the JVM user can write, e.g. `~/.bashrc`? no — sqlite creates a *new* file, or opens an existing one and runs `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`. Writing to an existing file that isn't SQLite → error. Creating arbitrary file at a chosen path = arbitrary file creation, and DDL execution against any existing SQLite DB the user can reach (e.g. another app's DB → schema tampering). CWE-22 (Improper Limitation of a Pathname) / CWE-427? Use **CWE-22**. Severity: **Low** (requires control of the operator's env var, i.e. already privileged-ish). Also the README frames env as trusted. I'll include as Low with the note it's an allowlist that doesn't constrain the path component.\n\n7. **Logout deletes the session row (revoke sets revoked=1, then prune deletes `revoked = 1`), so there's no revocation record and no post-logout audit trail.** Combined with `PRUNE_SQL` running on issue/validate paths. Also means session reuse after logout can't be detected (no \"used after revocation\" signal). CWE-778? Better **CWE-778 Insufficient Logging** — hmm, it's really an audit-trail gap: **CWE-778**. Severity **Low**. Actually, is it even a vuln? The token is unusable (row gone → SELECT finds nothing → invalid). It's an audit gap. Low/informational.\n\n8. **Per-source throttle counter is never cleared on success and `normalizeSourceId` buckets unprintable/oversized input into shared buckets; in the CLI the source is `cli:<user.name>`** — for a real HTTP deployment behind a shared NAT/proxy, all users share one 25-attempt/15-min budget → unauthenticated lockout of legitimate users by any single abuser (availability). CWE-770 (Allocation of Resources Without Limits or Throttling) → actually the flaw is coarse-grained throttle key = DoS amplification: **CWE-770** / **CWE-400**. Severity **Low** (documented tradeoff; sourceId must be server-observed per docs). Also the CLI sourceId is attacker-influenced? No — `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")`, local. Keep Low.\n\n9. **CSRF defence relies solely on `SameSite=Strict`** — documented as out of scope (no HTTP layer). Skip? It's mentioned in README as a known limit. I'll put it in §6 as reviewed-with-no-finding? Better: it's a real residual risk but explicitly documented and there's no HTTP layer. I'll include as Low? The instruction says report issues. Hmm. There's no HTTP layer to mount the attack; the token is delivered as a cookie string for a future layer. SameSite=Strict is a genuine mitigation but requires an explicit `Origin`/`Sec-Fetch-Site` check to be robust (Safari drops SameSite=None... no, Strict is well-implemented). I'll list under §6 with the caveat rather than inflate the count.\n\nAlso: `Mask.username` in the log for authentication failure logs a masked identifier — fine.\n\nAnother real one I should verify: **`AccountService.authenticate` calls `throttle.tryAcquire` which *bumps the counter even on success*, and `clearAccount` clears account scope only.** If authentication throws (e.g. SQLException in tx2) after `verified`, the account counter stays incremented — fine.\n\nAnd: **registration `insert` returns boolean on UNIQUE violation, and both email and username share one boolean** → attacker can probe? No, both give ACCEPTED... wait: register returns ACCEPTED always unless throttled/validation error. If the email is taken, it returns ACCEPTED (lie to prevent enumeration) — good, but the *caller* is told \"account created\" while nothing was created. That's a UX/semantic issue (CWE-778? no). It's deliberate enumeration resistance. §6.\n\nOne more genuinely valuable: **`updatePasswordHash` is a blind write with no optimistic-concurrency guard** — fold into SEC-01.\n\nAnd: **Argon2 memory ceiling `MAX_MEMORY_KIB = 1 GiB` with up to `MAX_ITERATIONS=20` and `p=16`** — parse bounds allow a stored hash up to 1 GiB × 20 passes × 16 threads. `verify` derives using the *stored* params. So a tampered/corrupt row (or an attacker with DB write) can force up to 1 GiB memory and 20 iterations per login attempt → memory-exhaustion DoS on a multi-tenant service. The bound was added to prevent the *unbounded* case, but 1 GiB × concurrent logins is still far above what's needed: the current *settings* are at most 1 GiB too, so why allow a stored hash to exceed the configured policy? The fix: clamp to `min(stored, config.settings().memoryKib())` or reject stored params above the *current configured* values. That's a nice, concrete finding: CWE-770 / CWE-400 (Uncontrolled Resource Consumption). Severity **Medium**. Requires DB-write to plant, but note the more realistic trigger: none without DB write... Actually there IS a no-privilege path? `needsRehash` returns true if stored < configured, and hash() uses configured. So all app-created hashes ≤ configured bounds. So exploiting needs DB tampering → already privileged. Downgrade to **Low**, framed as defense-in-depth: the anti-DoS ceiling is 16× the configured policy and applies per-attempt, so a corrupt row (disk corruption, replication bug, partial write) can OOM the service on that user's next login rather than failing closed cheaply. I'll make it Low. Actually README/design intent is \"hostile cost parameters from the database\" (there's a test). The test `refusesHostileCostParametersFromTheDatabase` presumably uses a value above 1 GiB. So the ceiling is the tested mitigation. 5 (sensitive data): the log file is created before the handler opens it so its mode can\n * be set to {@code 0600} -- the audit trail of who logged in from where is not readable by other\n * local users.
